========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um, it’s... it’s a lot of things, I guess, but mostly what brings me in is that I just can’t shake this feeling—this really constant, heavy feeling—that I’ve completely ruined things for everyone. And I mean everyone. It really started getting bad with work. I’m a computer support specialist, so my whole job is supposed to be fixing things for people, you know, clearing tickets, getting them back online so they can do their jobs. But I’ve just been falling so, so far behind. The queue just backs up, and I know my team is having to cover for me, which isn't fair to them at all because they have their own projects and their own stress. I'm just making their lives harder by not pulling my weight. 

And then, because I'm so behind and just... stuck, it spills over into everything else. Like the apartment situation. I've completely lost control of it. I don't even know how I let it slip this far, but it's just... it's a complete disaster, and it's not fair to anyone who has to deal with me or the mess I've made of my life. I just feel like I'm dragging everyone down with me. It’s like I’ve broken everything I touch lately, and honestly, I just sit there and feel like I deserve whatever bad thing happens next. If I lose my job, or if things get worse with the apartment, I just... I feel like it's exactly what I have coming to me for being such a burden to everyone who relied on me. I just can't stop thinking about how much I've messed everything up.

Yeah... yeah, that’s exactly it. It’s honestly one of the hardest parts to explain, because it’s not just that I’m bored, it’s... well, most days I wake up with this completely flat, heavy sadness. Before I even get out of bed, before my feet even hit the floor, it’s just sitting there on my chest. 

And usually, before all this started, I had these things I would do to sort of reset myself, you know? Ways to decompress. Like, I’ve always been really into music, so I’d put on a record. I have this whole vintage audio setup that I actually fixed up myself—I’ve always loved tinkering with electronics, taking old receivers apart, soldering new parts, just figuring out how things work. It was a great way to clear my head after staring at computer support tickets all day. Or cooking, too. I used to spend hours on the weekend just getting completely lost in cooking a really complicated recipe, prepping all the ingredients, getting the timing just right. It was a whole ritual.

But now... none of it reaches me anymore. I can put on my absolute favorite album, and it’s just noise in the room. I don’t feel the music at all, it's just sound waves. I look at my workbench and my tools, and it just makes me feel exhausted. I don't even want to touch any of it. It’s like there’s this thick glass wall between me and anything that used to make me feel alive or give me a sense of peace. I just sit there on the couch, staring at the turntable spinning, and I just feel that same heavy emptiness. It makes me feel even worse because I know I should like these things, I know they used to make me happy, but I just... I just don't. It all just feels completely pointless now.

Yeah, it’s... well, like I was kind of just saying, it’s really there from the second I open my eyes. It’s not like it comes and goes. I honestly wish it came in waves, because then at least I’d have a moment to catch my breath, you know? But no, it’s pretty much most of the day, every single day. 

Like I mentioned a minute ago, most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already just anchored to me. And you’d think that maybe as the day goes on, maybe by lunchtime, or after I finally log off from work, it would lift just a little bit. In the past, if I was having a rough week, I could at least look forward to the evening. I’d tell myself, 'Okay, just get through these support tickets, just clear the queue, and then you can put on a record, or pull out the soldering iron, or spend an hour cooking a proper meal.' I used to rely on those moments of relief. 

But now, because this feeling is just so constant, there aren't any breaks. Those things that used to reset me—the music, the electronics, the cooking—they just don’t reach me anymore, no matter what time of day I try them. It’s just this continuous, flat line from morning until night. I keep waiting for a break in the clouds, just a five-minute window where the weight lifts and I feel like myself again, but it doesn't happen. It just stays right there with me all day long.

If I lose my job, or if things get worse with the apartment, I just... I feel like it's exactly what I have coming to me for being such a burden to everyone who relied on me. I just can't stop thinking about how much I've messed everything up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things the way you used to, even activities that you once found pleasurable or interesting?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, that’s exactly it. It’s honestly one of the hardest parts to explain, because it’s not just that I’m bored, it’s... well, most days I wake up with this completely flat, heavy sadness. Before I even get out of bed, before my feet even hit the floor, it’s just sitting there on my chest. \n\nAnd usually, before all this started, I had these things I would do to sort of reset myself, you know? Ways to decompress. Like, I’ve always been really into music, so I’d put on a record. I have this whole vintage audio setup that I actually fixed up myself—I’ve always loved tinkering with electronics, taking old receivers apart, soldering new parts, just figuring out how things work. It was a great way to clear my head after staring at computer support tickets all day. Or cooking, too. I used to spend hours on the weekend just getting completely lost in cooking a really complicated recipe, prepping all the ingredients, getting the timing just right. It was a whole ritual.\n\nBut now... none of it reaches me anymore. I can put on my absolute favorite album, and it’s just noise in the room. I don’t feel the music at all, it's just sound waves. I look at my workbench and my tools, and it just makes me feel exhausted. I don't even want to touch any of it. It’s like there’s this thick glass wall between me and anything that used to make me feel alive or give me a sense of peace. I just sit there on the couch, staring at the turntable spinning, and I just feel that same heavy emptiness. It makes me feel even worse because I know I should like these things, I know they used to make me happy, but I just... I just don't. It all just feels completely pointless now.\n\nDoctor: Has this heavy, empty feeling been with you most of the day, nearly every day, or does it come and go in waves where you have moments of relief?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s... well, like I was kind of just saying, it’s really there from the second I open my eyes. It’s not like it comes and goes. I honestly wish it came in waves, because then at least I’d have a moment to catch my breath, you know? But no, it’s pretty much most of the day, every single day. \n\nLike I mentioned a minute ago, most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already just anchored to me. And you’d think that maybe as the day goes on, maybe by lunchtime, or after I finally log off from work, it would lift just a little bit. In the past, if I was having a rough week, I could at least look forward to the evening. I’d tell myself, 'Okay, just get through these support tickets, just clear the queue, and then you can put on a record, or pull out the soldering iron, or spend an hour cooking a proper meal.' I used to rely on those moments of relief. \n\nBut now, because this feeling is just so constant, there aren't any breaks. Those things that used to reset me—the music, the electronics, the cooking—they just don’t reach me anymore, no matter what time of day I try them. It’s just this continuous, flat line from morning until night. I keep waiting for a break in the clouds, just a five-minute window where the weight lifts and I feel like myself again, but it doesn't happen. It just stays right there with me all day long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Not really, no. I mean, honestly... it doesn't. Just to give you an example, um, last week my supervisor actually sent me a direct message. I guess I had finally managed to clear out this really old, complicated ticket that had been sitting in the queue forever. It was this weird network routing issue that had been bouncing around the tier 2 guys for weeks, and I somehow figured it out. Anyway, he sent this whole nice message in our main team chat, tagging me, saying 'great catch' and how much he appreciated it, and how it really helped the client out. 

And, you know, normally? A year ago? I would have been thrilled. I would have felt a little rush of pride, maybe even taken a screenshot of it to look at later if I was having a bad day. But when I read it... nothing. I just sat there staring at the monitor. It didn't make me feel lighter, it didn't make me smile, it didn't even make a tiny dent in that heavy feeling. If anything, my brain just immediately went to, 'Well, sure, you fixed this one thing, but look at the other fifty tickets you're completely ignoring, look at how much you're failing at everything else.' It’s like the good stuff just bounces right off me now. 

It happened with my mom, too, actually. She called on Sunday, and she was telling me about my sister, sending pictures of my niece, and she was just so happy and upbeat. And I just... I had to completely fake it. I had to force my voice to sound excited and use all this energy I didn't have just to pretend, because I didn't want to bring her down or make her worry. But inside, I was just completely flat. Just empty. It really doesn't matter what happens right now, honestly. A compliment at work, good news from my family, a sunny day... it's like my brain just can't process it as good anymore. It just gets swallowed up by that same heaviness.

But no, it’s pretty much most of the day, every single day. \n\nLike I mentioned a minute ago, most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already just anchored to me. And you’d think that maybe as the day goes on, maybe by lunchtime, or after I finally log off from work, it would lift just a little bit. In the past, if I was having a rough week, I could at least look forward to the evening. I’d tell myself, 'Okay, just get through these support tickets, just clear the queue, and then you can put on a record, or pull out the soldering iron, or spend an hour cooking a proper meal.' I used to rely on those moments of relief. \n\nBut now, because this feeling is just so constant, there aren't any breaks. Those things that used to reset me—the music, the electronics, the cooking—they just don’t reach me anymore, no matter what time of day I try them. It’s just this continuous, flat line from morning until night. I keep waiting for a break in the clouds, just a five-minute window where the weight lifts and I feel like myself again, but it doesn't happen. It just stays right there with me all day long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood changes at all in response to good things happening, like if something positive occurs in your day or if someone says something encouraging to you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, honestly... it doesn't. Just to give you an example, um, last week my supervisor actually sent me a direct message. I guess I had finally managed to clear out this really old, complicated ticket that had been sitting in the queue forever. It was this weird network routing issue that had been bouncing around the tier 2 guys for weeks, and I somehow figured it out. Anyway, he sent this whole nice message in our main team chat, tagging me, saying 'great catch' and how much he appreciated it, and how it really helped the client out. \n\nAnd, you know, normally? A year ago? I would have been thrilled. I would have felt a little rush of pride, maybe even taken a screenshot of it to look at later if I was having a bad day. But when I read it... nothing. I just sat there staring at the monitor. It didn't make me feel lighter, it didn't make me smile, it didn't even make a tiny dent in that heavy feeling. If anything, my brain just immediately went to, 'Well, sure, you fixed this one thing, but look at the other fifty tickets you're completely ignoring, look at how much you're failing at everything else.' It’s like the good stuff just bounces right off me now. \n\nIt happened with my mom, too, actually. She called on Sunday, and she was telling me about my sister, sending pictures of my niece, and she was just so happy and upbeat. And I just... I had to completely fake it. I had to force my voice to sound excited and use all this energy I didn't have just to pretend, because I didn't want to bring her down or make her worry. But inside, I was just completely flat. Just empty. It really doesn't matter what happens right now, honestly. A compliment at work, good news from my family, a sunny day... it's like my brain just can't process it as good anymore. It just gets swallowed up by that same heaviness.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had experiences like this before, where you felt this kind of heavy sadness and loss of pleasure, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / computer_support_specialist / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about a year and two months, ever since the pipe burst forced me into the smaller apartment and my routines fell apart.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I... I haven't really felt this way before. I mean, sure, I've had bad days, or maybe a stressful week at work where I just wanted to crash on the weekend, but nothing like this. This is entirely different. It's like a completely different animal. 

This whole thing, this... this heaviness and not caring about the music or the electronics or anything... it's been going on for a long time now. Like, about a year and two months, I think? Yeah, about fourteen months. And I can trace it back exactly to when it started, because it wasn't just a gradual thing, it was tied to this huge disruption in my life. 

See, I was living in this great place, had plenty of space for my workbench, all my audio gear was set up perfectly. I had my routines, you know? But then a pipe burst in the building. It was a complete disaster. Half my stuff got ruined, water everywhere, and the landlord basically said it would take months to fix and I had to get out. I had to scramble and move into this much smaller apartment across town. 

And that's when everything just... fell apart. In the new place, I didn't have room for my workbench. My vintage receiver is sitting in a box because there's no space to set it up properly. My whole routine, the way I used to decompress, the way I structured my weekends with cooking and tinkering—it all just vanished overnight. And ever since then, since that move a year and two months ago, I've just been stuck in this flat, empty place. I kept thinking I would adjust, that I'd unpack the boxes and find a new rhythm, but I never did. The boxes are still there, and this heavy sadness just moved in with me and never left. It's just been this endless, dragging year and a bit of feeling completely hollowed out.

be easier if I just didn’t wake up and nobody had to deal with me anymore.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the major depressive episode\n  manifestation: I never had those voices or the fixed fear about being watched at work before the move and the depression set in. They show up on the same bad stretches when I’m feeling low, guilty, and worn down, not when I’m doing okay.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’m still working, but I’ve had to ask for accommodations and extra review because I’m making documentation mistakes I normally wouldn’t. I also skip some board-game nights and leave the apartment less because keeping up with normal routines feels harder than it used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and two months, ever since the pipe burst forced me into the smaller apartment and my routines fell apart.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / computer_support_specialist / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Has this heavy, empty feeling been with you most of the day, nearly every day, or does it come and go in waves where you have moments of relief?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s... well, like I was kind of just saying, it’s really there from the second I open my eyes. It’s not like it comes and goes. I honestly wish it came in waves, because then at least I’d have a moment to catch my breath, you know? But no, it’s pretty much most of the day, every single day. \n\nLike I mentioned a minute ago, most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already just anchored to me. And you’d think that maybe as the day goes on, maybe by lunchtime, or after I finally log off from work, it would lift just a little bit. In the past, if I was having a rough week, I could at least look forward to the evening. I’d tell myself, 'Okay, just get through these support tickets, just clear the queue, and then you can put on a record, or pull out the soldering iron, or spend an hour cooking a proper meal.' I used to rely on those moments of relief. \n\nBut now, because this feeling is just so constant, there aren't any breaks. Those things that used to reset me—the music, the electronics, the cooking—they just don’t reach me anymore, no matter what time of day I try them. It’s just this continuous, flat line from morning until night. I keep waiting for a break in the clouds, just a five-minute window where the weight lifts and I feel like myself again, but it doesn't happen. It just stays right there with me all day long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood changes at all in response to good things happening, like if something positive occurs in your day or if someone says something encouraging to you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, honestly... it doesn't. Just to give you an example, um, last week my supervisor actually sent me a direct message. I guess I had finally managed to clear out this really old, complicated ticket that had been sitting in the queue forever. It was this weird network routing issue that had been bouncing around the tier 2 guys for weeks, and I somehow figured it out. Anyway, he sent this whole nice message in our main team chat, tagging me, saying 'great catch' and how much he appreciated it, and how it really helped the client out. \n\nAnd, you know, normally? A year ago? I would have been thrilled. I would have felt a little rush of pride, maybe even taken a screenshot of it to look at later if I was having a bad day. But when I read it... nothing. I just sat there staring at the monitor. It didn't make me feel lighter, it didn't make me smile, it didn't even make a tiny dent in that heavy feeling. If anything, my brain just immediately went to, 'Well, sure, you fixed this one thing, but look at the other fifty tickets you're completely ignoring, look at how much you're failing at everything else.' It’s like the good stuff just bounces right off me now. \n\nIt happened with my mom, too, actually. She called on Sunday, and she was telling me about my sister, sending pictures of my niece, and she was just so happy and upbeat. And I just... I had to completely fake it. I had to force my voice to sound excited and use all this energy I didn't have just to pretend, because I didn't want to bring her down or make her worry. But inside, I was just completely flat. Just empty. It really doesn't matter what happens right now, honestly. A compliment at work, good news from my family, a sunny day... it's like my brain just can't process it as good anymore. It just gets swallowed up by that same heaviness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had experiences like this before, where you felt this kind of heavy sadness and loss of pleasure, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: No, I... I haven't really felt this way before. I mean, sure, I've had bad days, or maybe a stressful week at work where I just wanted to crash on the weekend, but nothing like this. This is entirely different. It's like a completely different animal. \n\nThis whole thing, this... this heaviness and not caring about the music or the electronics or anything... it's been going on for a long time now. Like, about a year and two months, I think? Yeah, about fourteen months. And I can trace it back exactly to when it started, because it wasn't just a gradual thing, it was tied to this huge disruption in my life. \n\nSee, I was living in this great place, had plenty of space for my workbench, all my audio gear was set up perfectly. I had my routines, you know? But then a pipe burst in the building. It was a complete disaster. Half my stuff got ruined, water everywhere, and the landlord basically said it would take months to fix and I had to get out. I had to scramble and move into this much smaller apartment across town. \n\nAnd that's when everything just... fell apart. In the new place, I didn't have room for my workbench. My vintage receiver is sitting in a box because there's no space to set it up properly. My whole routine, the way I used to decompress, the way I structured my weekends with cooking and tinkering—it all just vanished overnight. And ever since then, since that move a year and two months ago, I've just been stuck in this flat, empty place. I kept thinking I would adjust, that I'd unpack the boxes and find a new rhythm, but I never did. The boxes are still there, and this heavy sadness just moved in with me and never left. It's just been this endless, dragging year and a bit of feeling completely hollowed out.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period in the past where you felt this level of sadness or loss of interest that lasted for several weeks or more, before this current episode started?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. No, like I was saying, it's really just been since the move. I mean, sure, I've had times in my life where I was down or stressed. Like, when I first started this job, the learning curve was pretty steep and I was working a lot of overtime. I'd come home exhausted and just want to sleep all weekend. And, you know, back in college, there were definitely stressful semesters. Finals week, pulling all-nighters, feeling completely overwhelmed by the workload. But the second the last exam was done, I'd bounce right back. I'd go out with friends, celebrate, feel that huge sense of relief. It was never this... this prolonged, heavy emptiness. 

So, no, before the pipe burst and I had to leave my old apartment fourteen months ago, I never experienced anything like this. I was always able to bounce back. If I had a bad week, it was just a bad week. It wasn't this endless, flat line where nothing brings me joy anymore. It's like a switch flipped when I had to pack up my life in a hurry and move into this cramped place, and I just haven't been able to flip it back since. It's completely unprecedented for me.

I don't even know how I let it slip this far, but it's just... it's a complete disaster, and it's not fair to anyone who has to deal with me or the mess I've made of my life. I just feel like I'm dragging everyone down with me. It’s like I’ve broken everything I touch lately, and honestly, I just sit there and feel like I deserve whatever bad thing happens next. If I lose my job, or if things get worse with the apartment, I just... I feel like it's exactly what I have coming to me for being such a burden to everyone who relied on me. I just can't stop thinking about how much I've messed everything up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things the way you used to, even activities that you once found pleasurable or interesting?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, that’s exactly it. It’s honestly one of the hardest parts to explain, because it’s not just that I’m bored, it’s... well, most days I wake up with this completely flat, heavy sadness. Before I even get out of bed, before my feet even hit the floor, it’s just sitting there on my chest. \n\nAnd usually, before all this started, I had these things I would do to sort of reset myself, you know? Ways to decompress. Like, I’ve always been really into music, so I’d put on a record. I have this whole vintage audio setup that I actually fixed up myself—I’ve always loved tinkering with electronics, taking old receivers apart, soldering new parts, just figuring out how things work. It was a great way to clear my head after staring at computer support tickets all day. Or cooking, too. I used to spend hours on the weekend just getting completely lost in cooking a really complicated recipe, prepping all the ingredients, getting the timing just right. It was a whole ritual.\n\nBut now... none of it reaches me anymore. I can put on my absolute favorite album, and it’s just noise in the room. I don’t feel the music at all, it's just sound waves. I look at my workbench and my tools, and it just makes me feel exhausted. I don't even want to touch any of it. It’s like there’s this thick glass wall between me and anything that used to make me feel alive or give me a sense of peace. I just sit there on the couch, staring at the turntable spinning, and I just feel that same heavy emptiness. It makes me feel even worse because I know I should like these things, I know they used to make me happy, but I just... I just don't. It all just feels completely pointless now.\n\nDoctor: Has this heavy, empty feeling been with you most of the day, nearly every day, or does it come and go in waves where you have moments of relief?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s... well, like I was kind of just saying, it’s really there from the second I open my eyes. It’s not like it comes and goes. I honestly wish it came in waves, because then at least I’d have a moment to catch my breath, you know? But no, it’s pretty much most of the day, every single day. \n\nLike I mentioned a minute ago, most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already just anchored to me. And you’d think that maybe as the day goes on, maybe by lunchtime, or after I finally log off from work, it would lift just a little bit. In the past, if I was having a rough week, I could at least look forward to the evening. I’d tell myself, 'Okay, just get through these support tickets, just clear the queue, and then you can put on a record, or pull out the soldering iron, or spend an hour cooking a proper meal.' I used to rely on those moments of relief. \n\nBut now, because this feeling is just so constant, there aren't any breaks. Those things that used to reset me—the music, the electronics, the cooking—they just don’t reach me anymore, no matter what time of day I try them. It’s just this continuous, flat line from morning until night. I keep waiting for a break in the clouds, just a five-minute window where the weight lifts and I feel like myself again, but it doesn't happen. It just stays right there with me all day long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood changes at all in response to good things happening, like if something positive occurs in your day or if someone says something encouraging to you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, honestly... it doesn't. Just to give you an example, um, last week my supervisor actually sent me a direct message. I guess I had finally managed to clear out this really old, complicated ticket that had been sitting in the queue forever. It was this weird network routing issue that had been bouncing around the tier 2 guys for weeks, and I somehow figured it out. Anyway, he sent this whole nice message in our main team chat, tagging me, saying 'great catch' and how much he appreciated it, and how it really helped the client out. \n\nAnd, you know, normally? A year ago? I would have been thrilled. I would have felt a little rush of pride, maybe even taken a screenshot of it to look at later if I was having a bad day. But when I read it... nothing. I just sat there staring at the monitor. It didn't make me feel lighter, it didn't make me smile, it didn't even make a tiny dent in that heavy feeling. If anything, my brain just immediately went to, 'Well, sure, you fixed this one thing, but look at the other fifty tickets you're completely ignoring, look at how much you're failing at everything else.' It’s like the good stuff just bounces right off me now. \n\nIt happened with my mom, too, actually. She called on Sunday, and she was telling me about my sister, sending pictures of my niece, and she was just so happy and upbeat. And I just... I had to completely fake it. I had to force my voice to sound excited and use all this energy I didn't have just to pretend, because I didn't want to bring her down or make her worry. But inside, I was just completely flat. Just empty. It really doesn't matter what happens right now, honestly. A compliment at work, good news from my family, a sunny day... it's like my brain just can't process it as good anymore. It just gets swallowed up by that same heaviness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had experiences like this before, where you felt this kind of heavy sadness and loss of pleasure, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: No, I... I had my routines, you know? But then a pipe burst in the building. It was a complete disaster. Half my stuff got ruined, water everywhere, and the landlord basically said it would take months to fix and I had to get out. I had to scramble and move into this much smaller apartment across town. \n\nAnd that's when everything just... fell apart. In the new place, I didn't have room for my workbench. My vintage receiver is sitting in a box because there's no space to set it up properly. My whole routine, the way I used to decompress, the way I structured my weekends with cooking and tinkering—it all just vanished overnight. And ever since then, since that move a year and two months ago, I've just been stuck in this flat, empty place. I kept thinking I would adjust, that I'd unpack the boxes and find a new rhythm, but I never did. The boxes are still there, and this heavy sadness just moved in with me and never left. It's just been this endless, dragging year and a bit of feeling completely hollowed out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in the past where you felt this level of sadness or loss of interest that lasted for several weeks or more, before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: No, like I was saying, it's really just been since the move. I mean, sure, I've had times in my life where I was down or stressed. Like, when I first started this job, the learning curve was pretty steep and I was working a lot of overtime. I'd come home exhausted and just want to sleep all weekend. But even then, if I had a free Saturday, I'd still eventually pull out my soldering iron or put on a record and feel better. I never lost interest in the things I loved for weeks on end. \n\nAnd, you know, back in college, there were definitely stressful semesters. Finals week, pulling all-nighters, feeling completely overwhelmed by the workload. But the second the last exam was done, I'd bounce right back. I'd go out with friends, celebrate, feel that huge sense of relief. It was never this... this prolonged, heavy emptiness. \n\nSo, no, before the pipe burst and I had to leave my old apartment fourteen months ago, I never experienced anything like this. I was always able to bounce back. If I had a bad week, it was just a bad week. It wasn't this endless, flat line where nothing brings me joy anymore. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Weight? Um, no, not really. I mean... my weight has always been pretty steady. I think I've hovered around the exact same number for the last five years or so. And even with everything that's happened, that hasn't really changed. 

I will say, though, *what* I eat has changed completely. I used to cook a lot, like I was saying before about my weekend routines. In the old place, I had this great kitchen setup, you know, plenty of counter space. I'd spend Sunday afternoons doing meal prep—making these big batches of chili or roasting a chicken and portioning it all out for the week. It was really part of how I decompressed and got ready for the work week. 

But now... well, in this new apartment, the kitchen is basically just a hallway. I barely have room to open the oven door without hitting the fridge, and honestly, half my good pots and pans are still in a box shoved under my bed because the cabinets are just ridiculously tiny. So I don't cook anymore. I mostly just eat out of pure necessity. It's totally mechanical. Like, I'll microwave a frozen dinner or just slap some turkey on bread. I don't really enjoy it, and honestly, half the time the food just kind of tastes like cardboard, but I still eat. My stomach growls, I put something in it so I can get through my shift at the help desk, and that's it. 

But despite all that, despite basically living on sandwiches and microwave meals for fourteen months, no, my weight hasn't fluctuated. My clothes all still fit exactly the same as they did before the move. I haven't had to tighten my belt or buy bigger pants or anything like that. I guess I'm just consuming the exact same amount of calories in boring, processed food as I used to in the good home-cooked stuff. So, yeah, no weight gain or loss, and I'm not really eating more or less... just eating because I'm supposed to, I guess.

Can you tell me what’s been bothering you or what brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, um, it’s... it’s a lot of things, I guess, but mostly what brings me in is that I just can’t shake this feeling—this really constant, heavy feeling—that I’ve completely ruined things for everyone. And I mean everyone. It really started getting bad with work. I’m a computer support specialist, so my whole job is supposed to be fixing things for people, you know, clearing tickets, getting them back online so they can do their jobs. But I’ve just been falling so, so far behind. The queue just backs up, and I know my team is having to cover for me, which isn't fair to them at all because they have their own projects and their own stress. I'm just making their lives harder by not pulling my weight. \n\nAnd then, because I'm so behind and just... stuck, it spills over into everything else. Like the apartment situation. I've completely lost control of it. I just feel like I'm dragging everyone down with me. It’s like I’ve broken everything I touch lately, and honestly, I just sit there and feel like I deserve whatever bad thing happens next. If I lose my job, or if things get worse with the apartment, I just... I feel like it's exactly what I have coming to me for being such a burden to everyone who relied on me. I just can't stop thinking about how much I've messed everything up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things the way you used to, even activities that you once found pleasurable or interesting?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, that’s exactly it. It’s honestly one of the hardest parts to explain, because it’s not just that I’m bored, it’s... well, most days I wake up with this completely flat, heavy sadness. Ways to decompress. Like, I’ve always been really into music, so I’d put on a record. I have this whole vintage audio setup that I actually fixed up myself—I’ve always loved tinkering with electronics, taking old receivers apart, soldering new parts, just figuring out how things work. It was a great way to clear my head after staring at computer support tickets all day. Or cooking, too. I used to spend hours on the weekend just getting completely lost in cooking a really complicated recipe, prepping all the ingredients, getting the timing just right. It was a whole ritual.\n\nBut now... none of it reaches me anymore. I can put on my absolute favorite album, and it’s just noise in the room. I don’t feel the music at all, it's just sound waves. I look at my workbench and my tools, and it just makes me feel exhausted. I don't even want to touch any of it. It’s like there’s this thick glass wall between me and anything that used to make me feel alive or give me a sense of peace. I just sit there on the couch, staring at the turntable spinning, and I just feel that same heavy emptiness. It makes me feel even worse because I know I should like these things, I know they used to make me happy, but I just... I just don't. It all just feels completely pointless now.\n\nDoctor: Has this heavy, empty feeling been with you most of the day, nearly every day, or does it come and go in waves where you have moments of relief?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s... well, like I was kind of just saying, it’s really there from the second I open my eyes. It’s not like it comes and goes. I honestly wish it came in waves, because then at least I’d have a moment to catch my breath, you know? But no, it’s pretty much most of the day, every single day. \n\nLike I mentioned a minute ago, most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already just anchored to me. And you’d think that maybe as the day goes on, maybe by lunchtime, or after I finally log off from work, it would lift just a little bit. In the past, if I was having a rough week, I could at least look forward to the evening. I’d tell myself, 'Okay, just get through these support tickets, just clear the queue, and then you can put on a record, or pull out the soldering iron, or spend an hour cooking a proper meal.' I used to rely on those moments of relief. \n\nBut now, because this feeling is just so constant, there aren't any breaks. Those things that used to reset me—the music, the electronics, the cooking—they just don’t reach me anymore, no matter what time of day I try them. It’s just this continuous, flat line from morning until night. I keep waiting for a break in the clouds, just a five-minute window where the weight lifts and I feel like myself again, but it doesn't happen. It just stays right there with me all day long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood changes at all in response to good things happening, like if something positive occurs in your day or if someone says something encouraging to you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, honestly... it doesn't. Just to give you an example, um, last week my supervisor actually sent me a direct message. I guess I had finally managed to clear out this really old, complicated ticket that had been sitting in the queue forever. It was this weird network routing issue that had been bouncing around the tier 2 guys for weeks, and I somehow figured it out. Anyway, he sent this whole nice message in our main team chat, tagging me, saying 'great catch' and how much he appreciated it, and how it really helped the client out. \n\nAnd, you know, normally? A year ago? I would have been thrilled. I would have felt a little rush of pride, maybe even taken a screenshot of it to look at later if I was having a bad day. But when I read it... nothing. I just sat there staring at the monitor. It didn't make me feel lighter, it didn't make me smile, it didn't even make a tiny dent in that heavy feeling. If anything, my brain just immediately went to, 'Well, sure, you fixed this one thing, but look at the other fifty tickets you're completely ignoring, look at how much you're failing at everything else.' It’s like the good stuff just bounces right off me now. \n\nIt happened with my mom, too, actually. She called on Sunday, and she was telling me about my sister, sending pictures of my niece, and she was just so happy and upbeat. And I just... I had to completely fake it. I had to force my voice to sound excited and use all this energy I didn't have just to pretend, because I didn't want to bring her down or make her worry. But inside, I was just completely flat. Just empty. It really doesn't matter what happens right now, honestly. A compliment at work, good news from my family, a sunny day... it's like my brain just can't process it as good anymore. It just gets swallowed up by that same heaviness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had experiences like this before, where you felt this kind of heavy sadness and loss of pleasure, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: No, I... I haven't really felt this way before. I mean, sure, I've had bad days, or maybe a stressful week at work where I just wanted to crash on the weekend, but nothing like this. This is entirely different. It's like a completely different animal. \n\nThis whole thing, this... this heaviness and not caring about the music or the electronics or anything... it's been going on for a long time now. Like, about a year and two months, I think? Yeah, about fourteen months. And I can trace it back exactly to when it started, because it wasn't just a gradual thing, it was tied to this huge disruption in my life. \n\nSee, I was living in this great place, had plenty of space for my workbench, all my audio gear was set up perfectly. I had my routines, you know? But then a pipe burst in the building. It was a complete disaster. Half my stuff got ruined, water everywhere, and the landlord basically said it would take months to fix and I had to get out. I had to scramble and move into this much smaller apartment across town. \n\nAnd that's when everything just... fell apart. In the new place, I didn't have room for my workbench. My vintage receiver is sitting in a box because there's no space to set it up properly. My whole routine, the way I used to decompress, the way I structured my weekends with cooking and tinkering—it all just vanished overnight. And ever since then, since that move a year and two months ago, I've just been stuck in this flat, empty place. I kept thinking I would adjust, that I'd unpack the boxes and find a new rhythm, but I never did. The boxes are still there, and this heavy sadness just moved in with me and never left. It's just been this endless, dragging year and a bit of feeling completely hollowed out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in the past where you felt this level of sadness or loss of interest that lasted for several weeks or more, before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: No, like I was saying, it's really just been since the move. I mean, sure, I've had times in my life where I was down or stressed. Like, when I first started this job, the learning curve was pretty steep and I was working a lot of overtime. I'd come home exhausted and just want to sleep all weekend. But even then, if I had a free Saturday, I'd still eventually pull out my soldering iron or put on a record and feel better. I never lost interest in the things I loved for weeks on end. \n\nAnd, you know, back in college, there were definitely stressful semesters. Finals week, pulling all-nighters, feeling completely overwhelmed by the workload. But the second the last exam was done, I'd bounce right back. I'd go out with friends, celebrate, feel that huge sense of relief. It was never this... this prolonged, heavy emptiness. \n\nSo, no, before the pipe burst and I had to leave my old apartment fourteen months ago, I never experienced anything like this. I was always able to bounce back. If I had a bad week, it was just a bad week. It wasn't this endless, flat line where nothing brings me joy anymore. It's like a switch flipped when I had to pack up my life in a hurry and move into this cramped place, and I just haven't been able to flip it back since. It's completely unprecedented for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight during this time—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Weight? Um, no, not really. I mean... my weight has always been pretty steady. I think I've hovered around the exact same number for the last five years or so. And even with everything that's happened, that hasn't really changed. \n\nI will say, though, *what* I eat has changed completely. I used to cook a lot, like I was saying before about my weekend routines. In the old place, I had this great kitchen setup, you know, plenty of counter space. I'd spend Sunday afternoons doing meal prep—making these big batches of chili or roasting a chicken and portioning it all out for the week. It was really part of how I decompressed and got ready for the work week. \n\nBut now... well, in this new apartment, the kitchen is basically just a hallway. I barely have room to open the oven door without hitting the fridge, and honestly, half my good pots and pans are still in a box shoved under my bed because the cabinets are just ridiculously tiny. So I don't cook anymore. I mostly just eat out of pure necessity. It's totally mechanical. Like, I'll microwave a frozen dinner or just slap some turkey on bread. I don't really enjoy it, and honestly, half the time the food just kind of tastes like cardboard, but I still eat. My stomach growls, I put something in it so I can get through my shift at the help desk, and that's it. \n\nBut despite all that, despite basically living on sandwiches and microwave meals for fourteen months, no, my weight hasn't fluctuated. My clothes all still fit exactly the same as they did before the move. I haven't had to tighten my belt or buy bigger pants or anything like that. I guess I'm just consuming the exact same amount of calories in boring, processed food as I used to in the good home-cooked stuff. So, yeah, no weight gain or loss, and I'm not really eating more or less... just eating because I'm supposed to, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I also skip some board-game nights and leave the apartment less because keeping up with normal routines feels harder than it used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and two months, ever since the pipe burst forced me into the smaller apartment and my routines fell apart.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / computer_support_specialist / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had experiences like this before, where you felt this kind of heavy sadness and loss of pleasure, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: No, I... I haven't really felt this way before. I mean, sure, I've had bad days, or maybe a stressful week at work where I just wanted to crash on the weekend, but nothing like this. This is entirely different. And I can trace it back exactly to when it started, because it wasn't just a gradual thing, it was tied to this huge disruption in my life. \n\nSee, I was living in this great place, had plenty of space for my workbench, all my audio gear was set up perfectly. I mean, sure, I've had times in my life where I was down or stressed. Like, when I first started this job, the learning curve was pretty steep and I was working a lot of overtime. I'd come home exhausted and just want to sleep all weekend. But even then, if I had a free Saturday, I'd still eventually pull out my soldering iron or put on a record and feel better. I never lost interest in the things I loved for weeks on end. \n\nAnd, you know, back in college, there were definitely stressful semesters. Finals week, pulling all-nighters, feeling completely overwhelmed by the workload. But the second the last exam was done, I'd bounce right back. I'd go out with friends, celebrate, feel that huge sense of relief. It was never this... this prolonged, heavy emptiness. \n\nSo, no, before the pipe burst and I had to leave my old apartment fourteen months ago, I never experienced anything like this. Um, no, not really. I mean... my weight has always been pretty steady. I think I've hovered around the exact same number for the last five years or so. And even with everything that's happened, that hasn't really changed. \n\nI will say, though, *what* I eat has changed completely. I used to cook a lot, like I was saying before about my weekend routines. In the old place, I had this great kitchen setup, you know, plenty of counter space. I'd spend Sunday afternoons doing meal prep—making these big batches of chili or roasting a chicken and portioning it all out for the week. It was really part of how I decompressed and got ready for the work week. \n\nBut now... well, in this new apartment, the kitchen is basically just a hallway. I barely have room to open the oven door without hitting the fridge, and honestly, half my good pots and pans are still in a box shoved under my bed because the cabinets are just ridiculously tiny. So I don't cook anymore. I mostly just eat out of pure necessity. It's totally mechanical. Like, I'll microwave a frozen dinner or just slap some turkey on bread. I don't really enjoy it, and honestly, half the time the food just kind of tastes like cardboard, but I still eat. My stomach growls, I put something in it so I can get through my shift at the help desk, and that's it. \n\nBut despite all that, despite basically living on sandwiches and microwave meals for fourteen months, no, my weight hasn't fluctuated. My clothes all still fit exactly the same as they did before the move. I haven't had to tighten my belt or buy bigger pants or anything like that. I guess I'm just consuming the exact same amount of calories in boring, processed food as I used to in the good home-cooked stuff. I know we kind of just touched on this, but... honestly, no. I really haven't. I'm absolutely certain about that. Because, you know, I've always been a pretty level-headed guy. You kind of have to be in my line of work. Doing computer support, you're dealing with frustrated people all day long—people who forgot their passwords for the tenth time, or spilled coffee on a company laptop and are panicking, or they're yelling at you because the server is slow and they think it's somehow your personal fault. It can be a real grind. If I was the type of person who got dragged down into these long, persistent funks for weeks on end, I would never have survived in IT this long. I would have burned out years ago. 

So, no, even when work was tedious, or if I just had a lull in my personal life where nothing exciting was happening, I never lost my baseline, you know? I never lost that drive to go home and tinker. Even on the most boring, run-of-the-mill weeks where absolutely nothing special happened, I still looked forward to Saturday morning. I’d still wake up, make a good cup of coffee, and actually want to hunt down vintage audio parts online or mess around with my equipment. I always had that... that spark, I guess you'd call it. 

It didn't matter if there was a specific stressful event or just a really dull, repetitive month, I always cared about my hobbies. People always joke about the grumpy IT guy stereotype, and sure, I can get annoyed or tired, but I never felt this complete lack of pleasure before. I never just stopped caring about the things I love for weeks at a time. It’s strictly been since that pipe burst and I lost my space. Before that? Never. Not once.

It was really part of how I decompressed and got ready for the work week. \n\nBut now... well, in this new apartment, the kitchen is basically just a hallway. I barely have room to open the oven door without hitting the fridge, and honestly, half my good pots and pans are still in a box shoved under my bed because the cabinets are just ridiculously tiny. So I don't cook anymore. I mostly just eat out of pure necessity. It's totally mechanical. Like, I'll microwave a frozen dinner or just slap some turkey on bread. I don't really enjoy it, and honestly, half the time the food just kind of tastes like cardboard, but I still eat. My stomach growls, I put something in it so I can get through my shift at the help desk, and that's it. \n\nBut despite all that, despite basically living on sandwiches and microwave meals for fourteen months, no, my weight hasn't fluctuated. My clothes all still fit exactly the same as they did before the move. I haven't had to tighten my belt or buy bigger pants or anything like that. I guess I'm just consuming the exact same amount of calories in boring, processed food as I used to in the good home-cooked stuff. So, yeah, no weight gain or loss, and I'm not really eating more or less... just eating because I'm supposed to, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this current one—lasting weeks or more—where you felt persistently down, lost interest in things, or struggled to feel pleasure, even if it wasn’t tied to a specific event like the move?\n\nPatient: I know we kind of just touched on this, but... honestly, no. I really haven't. I'm absolutely certain about that. Because, you know, I've always been a pretty level-headed guy. You kind of have to be in my line of work. Doing computer support, you're dealing with frustrated people all day long—people who forgot their passwords for the tenth time, or spilled coffee on a company laptop and are panicking, or they're yelling at you because the server is slow and they think it's somehow your personal fault. It can be a real grind. If I was the type of person who got dragged down into these long, persistent funks for weeks on end, I would never have survived in IT this long. I would have burned out years ago. \n\nSo, no, even when work was tedious, or if I just had a lull in my personal life where nothing exciting was happening, I never lost my baseline, you know? I never lost that drive to go home and tinker. Even on the most boring, run-of-the-mill weeks where absolutely nothing special happened, I still looked forward to Saturday morning. I’d still wake up, make a good cup of coffee, and actually want to hunt down vintage audio parts online or mess around with my equipment. I always had that... that spark, I guess you'd call it. \n\nIt didn't matter if there was a specific stressful event or just a really dull, repetitive month, I always cared about my hobbies. People always joke about the grumpy IT guy stereotype, and sure, I can get annoyed or tired, but I never felt this complete lack of pleasure before. I never just stopped caring about the things I love for weeks at a time. It’s strictly been since that pipe burst and I lost my space. Before that? Never. Not once.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore the move, before this current episode started, have you ever had any other periods in your life where you felt persistently down or lost interest in things for more than two weeks at a time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / computer_support_specialist / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the things that used to reset me—putting on a record, cooking, tinkering with electronics—just don’t reach me anymore.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like we're going in circles here a bit, Doc. Like I've been saying, no. Never. Not before the move. But since then... since I had to pack up my life and move into that shoebox, it's just... it's every day. Most days I wake up and there's just this... this flat, heavy sadness. It's like a weight on my chest before my feet even hit the floor. And the worst part is, the things that always used to reset me, you know? The things that kept me grounded. Like, I used to love just putting on a good vinyl record—maybe some early jazz or classic rock, something with a really warm analog sound—and just letting it wash over me. Or spending a whole Sunday afternoon cooking a big pot of chili, or pulling out my soldering iron and tinkering with some old stereo equipment I found at a thrift store. Those things used to be my anchor. They'd clear my head after a long week of dealing with network outages and password resets. But now? It's like they just... they don't reach me anymore. I look at my records and I just feel nothing. I look at my tools and it just seems like too much effort. It's just this constant, heavy flatness, day in and day out, and it's all been since the move. Before that, I was fine. I really was.

It’s not like it comes and goes. I honestly wish it came in waves, because then at least I’d have a moment to catch my breath, you know? But no, it’s pretty much most of the day, every single day. \n\nLike I mentioned a minute ago, most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness already just anchored to me. And you’d think that maybe as the day goes on, maybe by lunchtime, or after I finally log off from work, it would lift just a little bit. In the past, if I was having a rough week, I could at least look forward to the evening. I used to rely on those moments of relief. \n\nBut now, because this feeling is just so constant, there aren't any breaks. Those things that used to reset me—the music, the electronics, the cooking—they just don’t reach me anymore, no matter what time of day I try them. It’s just this continuous, flat line from morning until night. I keep waiting for a break in the clouds, just a five-minute window where the weight lifts and I feel like myself again, but it doesn't happen. It just stays right there with me all day long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood changes at all in response to good things happening, like if something positive occurs in your day or if someone says something encouraging to you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, honestly... it doesn't. Just to give you an example, um, last week my supervisor actually sent me a direct message. I guess I had finally managed to clear out this really old, complicated ticket that had been sitting in the queue forever. It was this weird network routing issue that had been bouncing around the tier 2 guys for weeks, and I somehow figured it out. Anyway, he sent this whole nice message in our main team chat, tagging me, saying 'great catch' and how much he appreciated it, and how it really helped the client out. \n\nAnd, you know, normally? A year ago? I would have been thrilled. I would have felt a little rush of pride, maybe even taken a screenshot of it to look at later if I was having a bad day. But when I read it... nothing. I just sat there staring at the monitor. It didn't make me feel lighter, it didn't make me smile, it didn't even make a tiny dent in that heavy feeling. If anything, my brain just immediately went to, 'Well, sure, you fixed this one thing, but look at the other fifty tickets you're completely ignoring, look at how much you're failing at everything else.' It’s like the good stuff just bounces right off me now. \n\nIt happened with my mom, too, actually. She called on Sunday, and she was telling me about my sister, sending pictures of my niece, and she was just so happy and upbeat. And I just... I had to completely fake it. I had to force my voice to sound excited and use all this energy I didn't have just to pretend, because I didn't want to bring her down or make her worry. But inside, I was just completely flat. Just empty. It really doesn't matter what happens right now, honestly. A compliment at work, good news from my family, a sunny day... it's like my brain just can't process it as good anymore. It just gets swallowed up by that same heaviness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had experiences like this before, where you felt this kind of heavy sadness and loss of pleasure, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: No, I... This is entirely different. I had my routines, you know? But then a pipe burst in the building. It was a complete disaster. Half my stuff got ruined, water everywhere, and the landlord basically said it would take months to fix and I had to get out. I had to scramble and move into this much smaller apartment across town. \n\nAnd that's when everything just... fell apart. In the new place, I didn't have room for my workbench. My vintage receiver is sitting in a box because there's no space to set it up properly. My whole routine, the way I used to decompress, the way I structured my weekends with cooking and tinkering—it all just vanished overnight. And ever since then, since that move a year and two months ago, I've just been stuck in this flat, empty place. I kept thinking I would adjust, that I'd unpack the boxes and find a new rhythm, but I never did. The boxes are still there, and this heavy sadness just moved in with me and never left. It's just been this endless, dragging year and a bit of feeling completely hollowed out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in the past where you felt this level of sadness or loss of interest that lasted for several weeks or more, before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: No, like I was saying, it's really just been since the move. I mean, sure, I've had times in my life where I was down or stressed. Like, when I first started this job, the learning curve was pretty steep and I was working a lot of overtime. I'd come home exhausted and just want to sleep all weekend. But even then, if I had a free Saturday, I'd still eventually pull out my soldering iron or put on a record and feel better. I never lost interest in the things I loved for weeks on end. \n\nAnd, you know, back in college, there were definitely stressful semesters. Finals week, pulling all-nighters, feeling completely overwhelmed by the workload. But the second the last exam was done, I'd bounce right back. I'd go out with friends, celebrate, feel that huge sense of relief. It was never this... this prolonged, heavy emptiness. \n\nSo, no, before the pipe burst and I had to leave my old apartment fourteen months ago, I never experienced anything like this. I was always able to bounce back. If I had a bad week, it was just a bad week. It wasn't this endless, flat line where nothing brings me joy anymore. It's like a switch flipped when I had to pack up my life in a hurry and move into this cramped place, and I just haven't been able to flip it back since. It's completely unprecedented for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight during this time—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Weight? Just empty. It really doesn't matter what happens right now, honestly. A compliment at work, good news from my family, a sunny day... it's like my brain just can't process it as good anymore. It just gets swallowed up by that same heaviness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had experiences like this before, where you felt this kind of heavy sadness and loss of pleasure, or is this the first time you've felt this way?\n\nPatient: No, I... I haven't really felt this way before. I mean, sure, I've had bad days, or maybe a stressful week at work where I just wanted to crash on the weekend, but nothing like this. This is entirely different. It's like a completely different animal. \n\nThis whole thing, this... this heaviness and not caring about the music or the electronics or anything... it's been going on for a long time now. Like, about a year and two months, I think? Yeah, about fourteen months. And I can trace it back exactly to when it started, because it wasn't just a gradual thing, it was tied to this huge disruption in my life. \n\nSee, I was living in this great place, had plenty of space for my workbench, all my audio gear was set up perfectly. I had my routines, you know? But then a pipe burst in the building. It was a complete disaster. Half my stuff got ruined, water everywhere, and the landlord basically said it would take months to fix and I had to get out. I had to scramble and move into this much smaller apartment across town. \n\nAnd that's when everything just... fell apart. In the new place, I didn't have room for my workbench. My vintage receiver is sitting in a box because there's no space to set it up properly. My whole routine, the way I used to decompress, the way I structured my weekends with cooking and tinkering—it all just vanished overnight. And ever since then, since that move a year and two months ago, I've just been stuck in this flat, empty place. I kept thinking I would adjust, that I'd unpack the boxes and find a new rhythm, but I never did. The boxes are still there, and this heavy sadness just moved in with me and never left. It's just been this endless, dragging year and a bit of feeling completely hollowed out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in the past where you felt this level of sadness or loss of interest that lasted for several weeks or more, before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: No, like I was saying, it's really just been since the move. I mean, sure, I've had times in my life where I was down or stressed. Like, when I first started this job, the learning curve was pretty steep and I was working a lot of overtime. I'd come home exhausted and just want to sleep all weekend. But even then, if I had a free Saturday, I'd still eventually pull out my soldering iron or put on a record and feel better. I never lost interest in the things I loved for weeks on end. \n\nAnd, you know, back in college, there were definitely stressful semesters. Finals week, pulling all-nighters, feeling completely overwhelmed by the workload. But the second the last exam was done, I'd bounce right back. I'd go out with friends, celebrate, feel that huge sense of relief. It was never this... this prolonged, heavy emptiness. \n\nSo, no, before the pipe burst and I had to leave my old apartment fourteen months ago, I never experienced anything like this. I was always able to bounce back. If I had a bad week, it was just a bad week. It wasn't this endless, flat line where nothing brings me joy anymore. It's like a switch flipped when I had to pack up my life in a hurry and move into this cramped place, and I just haven't been able to flip it back since. It's completely unprecedented for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight during this time—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Weight? Um, no, not really. I mean... my weight has always been pretty steady. I think I've hovered around the exact same number for the last five years or so. And even with everything that's happened, that hasn't really changed. \n\nI will say, though, *what* I eat has changed completely. I used to cook a lot, like I was saying before about my weekend routines. In the old place, I had this great kitchen setup, you know, plenty of counter space. I'd spend Sunday afternoons doing meal prep—making these big batches of chili or roasting a chicken and portioning it all out for the week. It was really part of how I decompressed and got ready for the work week. \n\nBut now... well, in this new apartment, the kitchen is basically just a hallway. I barely have room to open the oven door without hitting the fridge, and honestly, half my good pots and pans are still in a box shoved under my bed because the cabinets are just ridiculously tiny. I don't really enjoy it, and honestly, half the time the food just kind of tastes like cardboard, but I still eat. My clothes all still fit exactly the same as they did before the move. I haven't had to tighten my belt or buy bigger pants or anything like that. I guess I'm just consuming the exact same amount of calories in boring, processed food as I used to in the good home-cooked stuff. So, yeah, no weight gain or loss, and I'm not really eating more or less... just eating because I'm supposed to, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this current one—lasting weeks or more—where you felt persistently down, lost interest in things, or struggled to feel pleasure, even if it wasn’t tied to a specific event like the move?\n\nPatient: I know we kind of just touched on this, but... honestly, no. I really haven't. I'm absolutely certain about that. Because, you know, I've always been a pretty level-headed guy. You kind of have to be in my line of work. Doing computer support, you're dealing with frustrated people all day long—people who forgot their passwords for the tenth time, or spilled coffee on a company laptop and are panicking, or they're yelling at you because the server is slow and they think it's somehow your personal fault. I’d still wake up, make a good cup of coffee, and actually want to hunt down vintage audio parts online or mess around with my equipment. I always had that... that spark, I guess you'd call it. \n\nIt didn't matter if there was a specific stressful event or just a really dull, repetitive month, I always cared about my hobbies. People always joke about the grumpy IT guy stereotype, and sure, I can get annoyed or tired, but I never felt this complete lack of pleasure before. I never just stopped caring about the things I love for weeks at a time. It’s strictly been since that pipe burst and I lost my space. Before that? Never. Not once.\n\nDoctor: Before the move, before this current episode started, have you ever had any other periods in your life where you felt persistently down or lost interest in things for more than two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're going in circles here a bit, Doc. Those things used to be my anchor. They'd clear my head after a long week of dealing with network outages and password resets. But now? It's like they just... they don't reach me anymore. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sleep... yeah, sleep has become a whole ordeal, honestly. It’s funny you ask that because it’s been one of the most frustrating parts of this whole situation. I mean, in my old place, it was so quiet, you know? I’d just hit the pillow and be out. But this new apartment... I’ll get into bed around eleven, because I have to be logged in for the morning shift at the help desk by eight, but I don't actually sleep. 

I just... I’ll lie there for hours. Literally hours, just staring at the ceiling in the dark, listening. The walls are incredibly thin, so I'm just lying there listening for all these apartment noises. I hear the pipes clanking, the floorboards creaking from the guy upstairs pacing around, the hum of the refrigerator, the elevator dinging down the hall... it’s like my ears are just locked onto every little creak and groan the building makes. So I'm just waiting for the next sound, totally wired. 

And then, when I finally, finally manage to drift off—usually not until two or three in the morning—it’s not even a restful sleep. I end up waking up way before dawn, like 4:30 or 5:00 AM, and my whole body is just... tense. I wake up already clenched and completely exhausted. It’s exactly like... well, it's like a computer that never actually shut off. I never really powered down, you know? I'm just stuck in this awful standby mode where the internal fan is still spinning and the battery is just draining, but nothing is actually resting. So by the time my alarm actually goes off, I feel worse than when I laid down in the first place.
